Colors are brightness interpolation (light/dark cream squares and near-black ink) and do not represent a real board material. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.0022 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger (64 px per square); 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`graycodediscs` adds Gray-code encoder discs, bringing the current catalog from 947 to 948 kinds (949 gallery choices including plain). Each tile has four optical rotary-encoder discs, one per quadrant, with 3, 4, 5 and 6 concentric tracks (8, 16, 32 and 64 sectors): sectors are numbered from the +u direction toward +v (clockwise on screen), and track k (counted from the outside, so the outermost is the fastest-changing bit 0) is a bright clear slot where bit k of the sector's Gray code g = s XOR (s >> 1) is 1 and a dark opaque block where it is 0, so neighboring sectors (including the last one wrapping to the first) differ in exactly one track. The rim carries thin sector tick marks, there is a dark shaft hole in the center and a mid-grey mat outside the disc. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the track counts are fixed at 3, 4, 5 and 6 (the seed only changes the fine grain of the mat), tracks have equal width, and there are no photosensors, shields or shaft; bright for clear and dark for opaque is only a brightness convention, not an optical material; colors are brightness interpolation. Defaults are a 0.35 m nominal tile, 0.0028 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger (at 512 px a sector of the 64-sector disc is about 3 px wide at the outermost track); 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`code39barcodes` adds Code 39 barcodes, bringing the current catalog from 948 to 949 kinds (950 gallery choices including plain). Each tile has 6 rows of barcodes, each of the form `*XXXXX*`: the start/stop character `*` around 5 characters chosen by the seed (0–9, A–Z, `-`, `.`). Every character is 9 elements (5 black bars and 4 spaces, starting with a bar) of which exactly 3 are wide (3× the narrow width) and 6 narrow; characters are separated by one narrow space, with quiet zones of at least 10 narrow units before and after. The paper is cream, the bars carry slight ink density variation and grain. Metallic and mortar are 0.

The pattern table was typed from memory and decoded back independently in the tests using the structural rule described on Wikipedia's Code 39 page (two wide bars encode a number 1–10 with the weights 1, 2, 4, 7, 0; the one wide space out of four picks one of the groups U–Z plus symbols / digits / A–J / K–T), confirming that the patterns of all 38 data characters and of the `*` delimiter follow that rule; they were not checked against a real barcode scanner or the full text of ISO/IEC 16388, and the codes were never actually scanned.

This is an authored schematic: no Code 39 check digit (the symbology does not require one), none of the symbols `$`, `/`, `+`, `%` and the space character (it can be encoded but this alphabet does not use it), no human-readable text; the wide-to-narrow ratio is fixed at 3:1 and a unit is 1/132 of the tile, so very low resolutions (such as 32) cannot resolve the narrow bars. Defaults are a 0.3 m nominal tile, 0.0012 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger (a narrow unit is about 7.7 px); 512 still resolves it at about 3.9 px; no three-backend or cross-browser verification is claimed.

`amidakuji` adds an amidakuji (ghost-leg lottery), bringing the current catalog from 949 to 950 kinds (951 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is an amidakuji with 8 vertical lines and 10 rung levels: at each level the seed draws rungs between neighboring lines at random (about 45% chance, never two touching rungs on one level); a row on top is numbered with the start numbers 1 to 8 and a row at the bottom shows, under each line, the start that ends there — every start walks down its line and moves across whenever it meets a rung, so the bottom row is a permutation of the top. The label bands are grey, the digits are 3×5 dot matrix, the paper cream and the ink near-black. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the bottom labels are start numbers, not prize names; rungs sit at the centers of the 10 levels (not at varying heights like a hand-drawn one); no line is marked as the winning one and no walked path is highlighted; colors are brightness interpolation (near-black ink on cream paper). Defaults are a 0.3 m nominal tile, 0.0016 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger (about 64 px between lines); 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`featherbarbs` adds feather barbs, bringing the current catalog from 950 to 951 kinds (952 gallery choices including plain). Each tile holds the vanes of two feathers side by side (shafts at u = 1/4 and 3/4): a thin bright shaft sends rows of barbs out to both sides, sweeping toward the tail — along one barb v + 1.3·|u − shaft| is constant, so the barbs are the stripes of the phase (v + slope·|dx|) / period, 48 per tile vertically; each barb has its own length (about 0.21 ± 0.03 tile) which gives a ragged outer edge, the gaps between barbs are dark, the tone goes from light to dark from inside to outside, and each barb has a slightly different brightness. The longest barb (about 0.24 tile) is still shorter than the 0.25 tile from shaft to boundary, so each feather leaves a margin of at least about 0.01 tile at the boundaries, so no barbs touch across the tile seam or between the two feathers. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: only barbs and shaft are drawn — no barbule detail, no curved or twisted vanes, no feather outline or tip; the two feathers are exact mirror arrangements; colors are brightness interpolation (near-black ground, cream barbs) and do not represent real feather hues; the high-frequency stripes (about 20 px per period at 1024, barbs about 0.55 of a period wide) will alias at small sizes. Defaults are a 0.25 m nominal tile, 0.003 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`dartboardsectors` adds a dartboard, bringing the current catalog from 951 to 952 kinds (953 gallery choices including plain). Each tile holds one standard dartboard: a bull (radius 6.35 mm) and outer bull (15.9 mm) at the center, then 20 sectors in the usual order, clockwise from the top, 20, 1, 18, 4, 13, 6, 10, 15, 2, 17, 3, 19, 7, 16, 8, 11, 14, 9, 12, 5, each with a single area, a treble ring (99 to 107 mm) and a double ring (162 to 170 mm); sectors alternate black and cream (20 is black), the doubles and trebles of black sectors are a "red" tone and those of cream sectors a "green" tone, and a bright steel wire marks every boundary; outside the double ring is a dark number ring with the numbers as upright (not rotated) 3×5 dot-matrix digits placed in the direction of each sector, and outside the board a mid-grey mat. Dimensions are defined in millimetres and scaled to the tile (225 mm corresponds to 0.47 tile). Metallic and mortar are 0.

A candid account of the data: the overall diameter of 451 mm and the 20 sections were confirmed in this work from Wikipedia's "Darts" article; the ring radii (bull 6.35, outer bull 15.9, treble 99 to 107, double 162 to 170 mm) and the order of the numbers were typed from memory of the standard and were not compared item by item with the full WDF/DRA specification; for the order there is one known property used as a self-check — the absolute differences of adjacent numbers sum to 198 (computed in the tests, and it holds), with the 20 numbers distinct and summing to 210. This is an authored schematic: colors are brightness (red and green are just two different greys), and there is no board fibre, no dart holes, no numbers rotated along the ring and no 3D shading of the wires; the wire width (about 0.0016 tile) is illustrative, not the real gauge. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.0028 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`tictactoegames` adds tic-tac-toe, bringing the current catalog from 952 to 953 kinds (954 gallery choices including plain). Each tile holds four 3×3 boards (6×6 cells in all), each showing the position of a random game: X moves first, the two sides alternately fill the 9 cells in a random order chosen by the seed, stopping at the first line, otherwise after 3 to 9 plies (chosen per board), so every position is one that can occur in play: X has as many marks as O or one more, the two sides never both have a line, and a line exists only for the side that has just moved. Grid lines are drawn only between the cells of one board; X is two diagonals and O a ring, both in near-black ink. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the winning line, the move order and the players are not marked; there are no dividing lines between the four boards, so on the 6×6 grid they run together (the edge cells of each board have no outer frame); the X and O tones differ by only 0.08 of brightness; "can occur" follows the necessary conditions above and is not a full game-tree verification of each position. Defaults are a 0.3 m nominal tile, 0.002 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger (about 85 px per cell); 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`quasicrystalwaves` adds quasicrystal-style waves, bringing the current catalog from 953 to 954 kinds (955 gallery choices including plain). Five plane waves cos(2π(m·x) + φ) are summed, with integer wave vectors m = (6, 0), (2, 6), (−5, 4), (−5, −4), (2, −6) that point roughly in the five directions of a regular pentagon (neighboring angles within 10° of 72°, length ratio below 1.15) and phases φ chosen by the seed; the sum S goes through the monotonic map 0.5 + 0.5·tanh(0.7·S) to the brightness (height and roughness come from the same sum), giving an approximately five-fold symmetric picture of spots and pentagram-like structures. Because the wave vectors are integers the whole pattern is exactly periodic over the tile and repeats seamlessly. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: it is a periodic approximant, not a true quasicrystal — a real one (such as a Penrose tiling) has no translational period; the five-fold symmetry here is only approximate (the angle and length errors of rounding the vectors to integers) and the pattern still repeats at a distance; there is no diffraction and no physical quasicrystal meaning; colors are brightness interpolation (near-black navy to warm cream) and do not represent a particular palette. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.003 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 256 or larger (the highest spatial frequency is about 7.2 cycles per tile); 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`tallymarks` adds tally marks, bringing the current catalog from 954 to 955 kinds (956 gallery choices including plain). Each tile has 8 ruled rows, one count per row: on the left the usual "gate" tally in groups of five — four upright strokes crossed by a diagonal strike for the fifth — with a last, incomplete group showing only upright strokes (1 to 4), and on the right the row's total (3 to 49, chosen by the seed) printed in 3×5 dot-matrix digits. So the number of strokes in a row (a full group counting 5) equals the printed number. The ink varies slightly in density and the paper has a fine grain and ruled lines. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: it uses the four-strokes-and-a-strike grouping (not the East Asian 正 character count); every stroke is a perfect straight line with no hand-drawn wobble or width variation (the ink variation is only an overall brightness perturbation); at most 49 marks (10 groups) fit in a row. Defaults are a 0.3 m nominal tile, 0.0016 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger (strokes are about 3 px wide); 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`polargraphpaper` adds polar graph paper, bringing the current catalog from 955 to 956 kinds (957 gallery choices including plain). Each tile holds one centered polar chart: 12 equally spaced concentric circles (radius 0.04·k tile for k = 1 to 12, with the 5th, 10th and outermost heavier) and 36 radial lines every 10° (every 30° heavier and the four axes heaviest) running from the first circle out to the outer one, a small blank disc at the center, and plain paper outside the chart. The ink is a near-black teal and the paper cream with a fine grain. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: there are no angle or radius labels and no axis arrows; line widths are fixed fractions of the tile (thin 0.0011, heavy 0.0021, axes 0.0032) that do not adapt to the resolution, so at very low resolutions such as 32 the thin lines vanish; the chart is exactly one circle inside the tile, with no lines at the tile seam. Colors are brightness interpolation (dark teal to near-white). Defaults are a 0.3 m nominal tile, 0.0012 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger (thin lines about 2 px); no three-backend or cross-browser verification is claimed.

`chesterfieldtufting` adds Chesterfield diamond-tufted leather, bringing the current catalog from 956 to 957 kinds (958 gallery choices including plain). Each tile has a round button at each of the 8 grid points of a 4×4 grid whose index sum is even, with dark creases pulled diagonally between neighboring buttons and each diamond between the creases puffed up into a cushion. In the rotated coordinates s = 4(u + v), t = 4(u − v) the buttons sit at even integers and the creases are the lines s = 2m and t = 2m; inside a diamond the height is (sin(πs/2)·sin(πt/2))^0.7, highest at the middle and 0 on the creases. The brightness rises with the height from near-black at the creases to warm leather brown, the hide has small wrinkles and grain, and the buttons are small brighter discs with a rim. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: only the diamond tufting pattern — no button holes or stitches and no finer wrinkles radiating from the buttons; the creases are perfectly straight between buttons; the leather texture is brightness noise, not real pores; colors are brightness interpolation (near-black brown to brick brown) and do not represent a particular leather. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.02 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement, a strong relief) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`louvershutter` adds a louvered shutter, bringing the current catalog from 957 to 958 kinds (959 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is one shutter: a solid stile (about 7% wide) at each side, a vertical tilt rod down the middle and 10 tilted horizontal slats. Every slat is a sloped face — brighter along its top edge and darker toward the bottom — with a dark gap occupying the bottom 14% before the next slat; the rod stays visible through the gaps and a small round pin joins it to the middle of every slat. The slat surface has a fine paint grain stretched horizontally. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: a simplified head-on view with no perspective, no background or light showing through between the slats (the gap is just dark) and no real slat tilt angle — the tilt is expressed only by the bright-to-dark gradient and a sloped height; the stiles have no joinery or panels; colors are brightness interpolation (near-black grey to light grey-white) and do not represent a particular paint. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`ledgersheet` adds a ledger sheet, bringing the current catalog from 958 to 959 kinds (960 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a simple 8-row ledger: each row has its number (1 to 8) on the left, a right-aligned three-digit amount (100 to 999, chosen by the seed) in the middle column and a right-aligned running balance (the sum of all amounts so far — three digits in the first row, mostly four after that) in the right column; two darker vertical lines separate the columns, light horizontal rules separate the rows, the digits are 3×5 dot matrix, the ink has slight density variation and the cream paper a fine grain. So every row's balance equals the previous balance plus its amount. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: no column headings, currency signs, decimals or debit/credit columns — only the amount and running-balance number columns; the amount range is fixed at 100 to 999 with at most 8 rows (the largest balance is 7992, four digits); colors are brightness interpolation (near-black blue ink on cream paper) and do not represent real ledger ink colors. Defaults are a 0.3 m nominal tile, 0.0014 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger (a dot-matrix pixel is about 5 px); 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`sundialplate` adds a sundial plate, bringing the current catalog from 959 to 960 kinds (961 gallery choices including plain). Each tile holds the face of a horizontal sundial (a bronze disc of radius 0.475 tile on a dark brown mat): a small hub at the center where the gnomon would stand and 13 hour lines fanning out over the upper half, the noon line vertical and heavier, the line h hours from noon making the angle atan(sin φ · tan(15°·h)) with it for h = −6 to 6 (the ±6 hour lines are horizontal), where φ is the latitude, chosen by the seed between 25° and 55°. There are three concentric rings (radius 0.2, 0.3 and 0.385 tile) and the hour numerals (6, 7, …, 11, 12, 1, …, 6 in 3×5 dot matrix) beyond the end of each line; the numerals of neighboring hours alternate between two radii so that the ones near noon do not overlap at low latitudes. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: only the face is drawn, with no 3D gnomon or its shadow; there is no longitude correction, equation of time or daylight saving; each seed is a different latitude, not a particular city; there are no half-hour or finer divisions; colors are brightness interpolation (near-black brown to bronze) and do not represent the patina of real bronze. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.003 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`doubleslitfringes` adds double-slit interference fringes, bringing the current catalog from 960 to 961 kinds (962 gallery choices including plain). Each tile stacks four far-field double-slit patterns whose intensity along the horizontal is I(x) = cos²(πx/p) · sinc²(πx/(r·p)), with x the offset from the center, p = 1/16 tile the fringe period and r the slit separation divided by the slit width; the four panels use r = 2, 3, 4, 5: the fringes sit at whole multiples of p, and when the order m is a multiple of r it falls exactly on a zero of the single-slit sinc² envelope and is missing. The brightness is I^0.6, on a dark background with cream fringes and a thin dark gap between panels. The pattern is mirror symmetric about the center, so it is continuous at the tile seam and repeats seamlessly. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: it is the idealized far-field (Fraunhofer) formula, not an experimental image — no finite screen distance, finite source width or non-monochromatic blurring of the fringes, and no physical intensity units; the pattern is one-dimensional along the horizontal and merely repeated vertically; the seed only changes a very fine grain; colors are brightness (near-black blue to cream) and do not represent a particular wavelength of light. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.002 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger (the fringe period is about 32 px); 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`fibonaccisquares` adds Fibonacci squares with a golden spiral, bringing the current catalog from 961 to 962 kinds (963 gallery choices including plain). In the middle of each tile is a 13 × 21 unit rectangle (unit = 1/24 tile) tiled by seven squares of side 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8 and 13 (aspect ratio 21/13 ≈ 1.615, close to the golden ratio; the areas 1 + 1 + 4 + 9 + 25 + 64 + 169 = 273 = 13 × 21 fill it exactly); each square is attached to one side of the current rectangle in the order right, top, left, bottom and has its own brightness; inside every square a quarter circle of radius equal to its side is centered on one of its corners, and neighboring arcs are tangent at their shared end point, forming one continuous golden spiral. Around the rectangle is cream paper. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the spiral is the Fibonacci-square approximation (quarter-circle arcs joined tangentially, with curvature jumping at the joints), not a true logarithmic spiral; the rectangle does not fill the tile and leaves blank margins, so there are gaps between the figures when the tile repeats; only seven squares (the largest 13) are drawn and it cannot be extended; the seed only changes the paper grain. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.0016 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger (the smallest square is about 21 px); 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`bluenoisemask` adds a blue-noise mask, bringing the current catalog from 962 to 963 kinds (964 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a 64×64 blue-noise dither mask built with the void-and-cluster algorithm (Ulichney): on a torus (edges wrapping) a Gaussian energy (σ = 1.5) finds the "tightest cluster" and the "largest void"; about 10% random initial points are relaxed to a stable pattern, then all 4096 texels are ranked 0 to 4095 with every rank used exactly once; brightness = rank ÷ 4095, so the brightness histogram is exactly flat and low-frequency energy is strongly suppressed. The mask is computed on a torus and repeats seamlessly; height rises with brightness and roughness rises slightly. Metallic and mortar are 0.

One-off local measurement (seed 0, discrete Fourier power of the 64×64 mask divided by the power expected for white noise): about 0.000 for frequencies of radius 1 to 5, about 0.003 for radius 8 to 12 and about 1.61 for radius 24 to 31 (near Nyquist); a random permutation of the same variance (white noise) gives about 1.21, 1.14 and 0.96. Creating a sampler takes about 66 ms (one-off, not a benchmark).

This is an authored product with limits: the mask size is fixed at 64×64 texels (larger would make the algorithm too slow) and it is sampled nearest-neighbour, so on a 512 px image each texel is an 8×8 px block (for texel-exact blue noise set the size to 64 or sample it with repeat in a shader); the seed only chooses the initial random points — different seeds give different masks of similar spectral quality; colors are brightness with no hue meaning. Defaults are a 0.2 m nominal tile, 0.002 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. No three-backend or cross-browser verification is claimed.

`elementaryautomata` adds one-dimensional elementary cellular automata, bringing the current catalog from 963 to 964 kinds (965 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is 2×2 panels of 31×31 cells, each evolving one Wolfram rule from top to bottom — rule 90 top left (Sierpinski triangle), 110 top right, 150 bottom left and 184 bottom right (the traffic rule): row 0 is the middle cell plus a sparse seed-chosen scatter of about 12%, and each next row is computed from the previous one by the rule (on a ring of 31 cells, the ends wrapping). Live cells are small near-black squares, dead cells small light-grey squares, a thin line separates the panels and the paper is cream. Because the ring has 31 cells (odd), rule 90 does not die out to all zeros after a few steps the way it does with 32 cells; the pattern continues to the bottom. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: only four rules, a fixed width of 31 cells and 31 rows; the ring boundary makes the pattern wrap around the left and right edges (it does not continue into the neighboring panels); the first row contains random points, so it is not the classic single-seed triangle (set seedDensity to 0 for a single seed); "an odd ring does not die out" was observed on the seeds tested here and has no mathematical proof; colors are brightness (near-black ink, light-grey grid, cream paper). Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.0014 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger (about 8 px per cell); 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`mastermindboard` adds a Mastermind board, bringing the current catalog from 964 to 965 kinds (966 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a 6-column × 9-row board: the top row is the revealed secret code (4 pegs chosen at random from 6 colors) and each of the 8 rows below is a random guess of 4 pegs; to the right of each guess are two columns forming a 2×2 block of feedback holes scored by the rules — a "exact" peg (right color in the right place, bright, 0.98) is placed first, then a "partial" peg (right color in the wrong place, near-black, 0.04), and the remaining holes stay empty — filled left to right, top to bottom. The 6 peg colors are six brightnesses (0.34 to 0.9, 0.12 apart), the holes are dark brown and the board is wood brown with a grain stretched vertically; a line separates the code row from the guesses. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the guesses are random, not reasoned from earlier feedback (so later guesses need not agree with earlier feedback, and no row is guaranteed to be a complete hit); there is no cover hiding the code; colors are only brightness and the six peg colors are hard to tell apart at small sizes; the feedback pegs' conventional "black/white" for exact/partial is shown here as bright/dark. Defaults are a 0.35 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`wordsearchgrid` adds a word search, bringing the current catalog from 965 to 966 kinds (967 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a 12×12 letter grid printed with 3×5 dot-matrix capitals from only 12 letters, A C D E G I L M N O R T: 5 words are picked by the seed from a list of 15 (CODE, TREE, GRID, MINT, TIDE, LION, DOOR, CLOT, RAIN, CELL, MOON, TIGER, ANGLE, CIRCLE, TRIANGLE) and placed at random in the eight directions (horizontal, vertical and diagonal, forwards or backwards; crossing an already placed letter only where the letters agree), and the remaining cells are filled with random letters from the alphabet. Light grey grid lines, cream paper, ink with slight density variation. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the word list is not printed on the tile; the random filler letters may accidentally spell other words from the list (the tests only require that at least 5 words of the list can be found in straight lines in the grid); only 12 letters are used and 3×5 dot-matrix letters are hard to tell apart at small sizes (for example D and O, M and N); there are no spaces or punctuation. Defaults are a 0.35 m nominal tile, 0.0014 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger (a cell is about 85 px and a dot-matrix pixel about 14 px); 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`magicsquares` adds magic squares, bringing the current catalog from 966 to 967 kinds (968 gallery choices including plain). Each tile holds one magic square per quadrant: order 3 (Lo Shu), order 4 (Dürer's square), and orders 5 and 7 (the odd orders by the Siamese method: start in the middle of the top row, move up and right, and step down one row when the cell is taken); each square is rotated 0 to 3 times by the seed and possibly reflected (rotation and reflection keep the property). So the numbers of each square are exactly 1 to n² once each, and every row, column and both diagonals sum to the magic constant n(n²+1)/2 (15, 34, 65, 175). Numbers are 3×5 dot matrix (one or two digits) with grid lines and a heavier outer frame on cream paper. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the numbers of the Lo Shu and Dürer squares were typed from memory and verified by the condition "every row, column and diagonal sums to the constant and the numbers are 1 to n²" (other properties of Dürer's square, such as the four corners and the central 2×2 also summing to 34, are not tested); the order-5 and order-7 squares come from the algorithm and are not particular traditional squares; there is no order-6 or other even order (they need different constructions); the order-7 cells are small (about 0.07 tile — about 36 px at 512, and a two-digit dot-matrix pixel is only about 4 px). Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.0014 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`slidingpuzzle` adds a sliding puzzle (the 15-puzzle), bringing the current catalog from 967 to 968 kinds (969 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a 4×4 board: 15 cream tiles with dot-matrix numbers 1 to 15 and one sunken empty square, inside a dark frame. The board starts from the solved state and is shuffled by 240 seeded legal slides (the empty square swaps with a neighbour, never immediately undoing the previous move), so it can always be solved again; tiles have thin gaps and bevels (height) and the numbers are slightly recessed. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: 240 random slides do not guarantee the board is as far from solved as possible and may leave a few tiles at home (the tests only require at most 6 in place); it is the result of legal slides, not a uniformly random permutation; the numbers are only 3×5 dot matrix and a two-digit number is about 0.7 cell wide; there is no animation or solver. Defaults are a 0.3 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`kaleidoscope` adds a kaleidoscope, bringing the current catalog from 968 to 969 kinds (970 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a 2×2 set of round rosettes with 6-, 8-, 5- and 12-fold dihedral symmetry (n-fold rotation plus n mirror axes): the pattern of each disc is 9 random dots inside a single wedge of angle π/n (radius, polar position and one of six brightnesses set by the seed) over concentric ring shading that depends on the radius; sampling folds the angle into the wedge (rotate by 2π/n, then mirror about the wedge edge), so each whole disc is exactly mirror- and rotation-symmetric. A dark rim surrounds each disc on a dark blue-black background. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: a real kaleidoscope mirrors real objects, this is a computed mirroring; the 12-fold disc looks almost radial because its wedge is narrow; the pattern is not a meaningful mandala; the discs sit at fixed positions (2×2) and do not move with the seed. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.002 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`oghaminscription` adds an Ogham inscription, bringing the current catalog from 969 to 970 kinds (971 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a rough grey stone face (fine-grained and coarse brightness variation) carved with eight horizontal rows of Ogham: each row has a horizontal stem line and a word is written left to right as letters of one to five short strokes. The four aicmi (groups) are drawn differently: group 1 (B L F S N) hangs below the stem, group 2 (H D T C Q) rises above it, group 3 (M G NG Z R) crosses the stem diagonally and group 4 (A O U E I, the vowels) is short notches on the stem; the number of strokes (1 to 5) picks the letter within the group. Each row's word is chosen by the seed from 16 Old Irish tree names (BETH, LUIS, FEARN, SAILLE, NION, UATH, DUIR, TINNE, COLL, CERT, GORT, RUIS, AILM, ONN, EADHA, IDHO); the carving is darker than the stone and recessed (lower height). Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the Ogham letter order (BLFSN / HDTCQ / MGNGZR / AOUEI) and the four-group stroke layout were typed from memory and not checked against a source; real Ogham is read from bottom to top along the edge of a stone, whereas this is a simplified horizontal left-to-right layout with each stem line running across the whole tile; the words are tree names spelled letter by letter (not a researched inscription, and the NG letter is not used — the test alphabet writes it as X); there is no weathering or chipping. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.006 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`gameoflife` adds Conway's Game of Life, bringing the current catalog from 970 to 971 kinds (972 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is split into 2×2 cell boards of 20×20 which, in reading order (top-left, top-right, bottom-left, bottom-right), are four consecutive generations of Conway's Game of Life: the seed makes a random initial state of density 0.36, runs it for 10 generations on a torus (edges wrap), and four consecutive generations by the rule — a live cell with 2 or 3 live neighbours survives, a dead cell with exactly 3 is born — fill the quadrants, so each board is the next generation of the one before. Live cells are bright green squares, dead cells dark green, with lighter grid lines and frames on a near-black background. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the start is a random soup, not a particular pattern (no gliders or other structures are placed on purpose); the boards are only 20×20 (torus); the burn-in is 10 generations instead of a longer one because with 24 generations as few as 21 live cells were left among 14 seeds (with my first one-dimensional hash for the soup even seed 0 was left with 8, and large empty areas appeared, so a two-dimensional hash is used), whereas 10 generations gives 59 to 121 live cells out of 400; the opposite edges of each board connect on the torus, but this wrap is not drawn on the tile. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`pascalmodulo` adds Pascal's triangle modulo n, bringing the current catalog from 971 to 972 kinds (973 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is split into 2×2 left-aligned 32-row Pascal triangles: the c-th square of row r follows the recurrence (each cell is the sum of the two cells above it) reduced modulo 2, 3, 4 and 5, one modulus per quadrant, with the seed choosing which modulus goes in which quadrant. A larger residue is a brighter square (brightness evenly spread between 0.2 and 0.94), the area above the diagonal is dark empty background, and each board has a thin frame. The modulo-2 board is the Sierpiński triangle. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: only 32 rows are shown; residues are brightness, not hue (the five levels for modulo 5 are about 0.185 apart and are hard to tell apart at small sizes); residue 0 is not "empty" but a darker square (brightness 0.2) — empty is only above the diagonal; each of the four moduli appears once. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.003 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`dragoncurve` adds a dragon curve, bringing the current catalog from 972 to 973 kinds (974 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is an order-11 Heighway dragon curve (the curve obtained by folding a strip of paper in half 11 times and unfolding it): 2048 unit-length lattice segments whose turn sequence is the regular paper-folding sequence (each round keeps the earlier turns, adds one left turn, then the earlier turns reversed and swapped). The curve is drawn as a thin line along the lattice with square joints at the corners and a brightness gradient along the path from dim orange to bright orange (0.42 at the start, 0.96 at the end) on a near-black deep purple background. The curve's rotation (0 to 3 quarter turns) and whether it is mirrored are set by the seed, and the whole curve is scaled to fit and centred in the tile with a margin. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: there is a single dragon curve whose shape does not vary with the seed (only its orientation and mirroring); it occupies the middle of the tile with flat background around it and does not fill the tile; the line width is about 0.34 of the lattice spacing, which at order 11 is about 0.02 tile — only about 3.5 px wide at 512 px; the order-11 curve's segments never overlap but do meet at vertices (so the joint squares can look like crossings). Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.003 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`cantordust` adds Cantor dust, bringing the current catalog from 973 to 974 kinds (975 gallery choices including plain). The top half of the tile is six rows of the Cantor set from top to bottom: row k (k = 0 to 5) has 243 cells and keeps those whose first k base-3 digits are none equal to 1 (row 0 is the whole strip, row 1 drops the middle third, and so on), with the kept segments brightening from row to row (0.42 to 0.92). The bottom half is three steps of Cantor dust: a 27×27 grid in which only the cells whose column and row indices both have no base-3 digit 1 (8×8 = 64) are lit, on a lighter square base. The background is dark blue-black with faint grain. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the Cantor set stops at step 5 (243 cells, each about 4 px wide at 1024 px) and the dust at 3 steps (27×27) — the real Cantor set is the limit of infinitely many steps; the middle third is always removed (I first let the seed choose which digit to remove, but removing digit 2 pushed all of the dust into a corner where it was hard to see, so it is fixed to 1), and the seed changes only the grain; it is not a visual proof about the fractal. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.003 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`eightqueens` adds the eight queens puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 974 to 975 kinds (976 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is split into 2×2 chessboards of 8×8 alternating light and dark squares in a dark wooden frame, and each board carries eight mutually non-attacking queens — one in every row and column and no two on a shared diagonal. A queen is a bright silhouette of a round body with a three-spike crown; each of the four boards shuffles its column order with the seed and runs a backtracking search, so the four are independent solutions (there are 92 solutions in all). Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the queens are simplified silhouettes, not 3-D pieces; the solutions of the four boards come from the seed-shuffled search and are not drawn uniformly from the 92, and two boards can coincide by chance; there are no coordinate labels; the squares are two brightnesses, not hues. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`hanoitowers` adds the Tower of Hanoi, bringing the current catalog from 975 to 976 kinds (977 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is split into 2×2 wooden-framed panels, each with three pegs and six disks of different sizes, and in reading order the four panels show the optimal solution (moving all disks from the left peg to the right one in 63 moves) after 0, 21, 42 and 63 moves: move 0 has all six disks stacked by size on the left peg and the last panel has them on the right peg. Larger disks are brighter and wider (0.08 to 0.30 of the panel width) and the pegs and base are a darker wood colour. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: there is one fixed optimal solution and fixed move counts (0, 21, 42, 63); the seed changes only the wood grain; the disks are told apart only by width and brightness, with no depth; the disk brightness is a grayscale between 0.38 and 0.96 (not hue) and the smallest disks are hard to tell apart at small sizes; no disk is drawn mid-move. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.006 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`latinsquares` adds Latin squares, bringing the current catalog from 976 to 977 kinds (978 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is split into 2×2 squares of n×n whose orders in reading order are 5, 6, 7 and 8: every cell is one of n brightness levels (evenly spread between 0.16 and 0.96) and every level occurs exactly once in each row and each column. The squares are the cyclic Latin square (i+j mod n) with its rows, columns and symbols shuffled by the seed, with thin gaps between cells, a thin frame and a dark blue-violet background. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: every square comes from the cyclic group's addition table with row, column and symbol permutations (so each is isotopic to the cyclic square) and the set does not cover all Latin squares of that order (order 6, for example, has many non-cyclic types); brightness levels are not hues, and adjacent levels of order 8 differ by only about 0.114, which is hard to tell apart at small sizes; there are no digits or letters. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.003 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`mayansums` adds Maya addition, bringing the current catalog from 977 to 978 kinds (979 gallery choices including plain). In each of the four quadrants of the tile a sum a + b = c is carved on a rough grey stone, with the numbers in Maya base-20 notation: each number is two stacked cells (the upper one the 20s digit, the lower one the units digit, each 0 to 19), and inside a cell 0 is an elliptical shell ring, 1 to 4 is a row of dots, 5 is a bar, and 6 to 19 is some bars (each worth 5) stacked at the bottom with a row of dots (each worth 1) above them. The seed picks a and b between 10 and 199 and c = a + b (at most 398, still a two-digit base-20 number); a plus and an equals sign are carved between the three columns. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the numerals are a simplified drawing of Maya base-20 digits (bars, dots, shell zero), not researched glyph forms, and the 18-based position of the Long Count calendar (where the second place only goes to 17) is not used; the sums are drawn only from the range 10 to 199 and the upper 20s cell can be 0 (a shell); the carving is just a recess darker than the stone, without weathering or chipping. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.006 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`sortingnetworks` adds sorting network diagrams, bringing the current catalog from 978 to 979 kinds (980 gallery choices including plain). In each of the four quadrants of the tile an 8-wire sorting network is drawn: eight horizontal wires (0 to 7 from top to bottom) and each comparator is a bright vertical bar with a dot at each end joining the two wires it compares (the smaller value goes to the upper wire). The seed picks one of two networks per quadrant: Batcher's odd-even merge sort (19 comparators) or the bitonic sorter (24 comparators, in its all-ascending form); every comparator gets its own column, left to right in execution order. A dark blue panel with faint grain and a thin frame. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: only 8 wires and two networks; to keep nested comparators from overlapping, each comparator has a column to itself (so the comparators of one layer are not drawn in a shared column as in the standard layered drawing); the test reads the comparator network back and checks with the 0-1 principle that it sorts all 256 binary inputs, but does not show that it has the fewest possible comparators. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.003 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`primesieve` adds a prime sieve, bringing the current catalog from 979 to 980 kinds (981 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a 16×16 board of rounded squares holding 256 consecutive integers row by row, left to right and top to bottom, starting at a number the seed picks between 1 and 745. Primes are the brightest yellow squares and are raised; composites are shaded by their smallest prime factor into five grey-yellow levels (dark to bright) for 2, 3, 5, 7 and 11 or more; the number 1 (if the window contains it) is the darkest square. The primes therefore sit exactly where the sieve of Eratosthenes leaves gaps, and multiples of 2 show up as dark vertical stripes every other column. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: there are no digits on the tile, so the numbers can only be inferred from the pattern; the smallest prime factors of 11 or more share one level (11, 13, 17… are not separated); the largest start is 745, so the window goes up to 1000 (the largest smallest prime factor is 31); brightness is grayscale, not hue; primality is decided by trial division, not by implementing the sieve. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`knockoutbracket` adds a knockout tournament bracket, bringing the current catalog from 980 to 981 kinds (982 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is the tree of a 16-team single-elimination tournament: on the left, round one is teams 1 to 16 shuffled by the seed (dot-matrix numbers, two glyphs for two digits), and to the right come the quarter-finals, semi-finals, final and champion as five columns of boxes, with the two boxes of each match joined by lines into the next column, whose box holds the winner of that match (the seed randomly picks the upper or the lower team), so every winner's number equals one of the two boxes that feed it, and the rightmost, brighter box is the champion. Light boxes, dark numbers and grey connectors on a dark blue background. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the results are entirely random and do not represent strength or a score; there are no scores, team names or dates; it is fixed at 16 teams with no byes or third-place match; the numbers are 3×5 dot matrix and a two-digit number is about 0.05 tile wide. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`skyscraperpuzzles` adds Skyscraper puzzles, bringing the current catalog from 981 to 982 kinds (983 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is split into 2×2 solved Skyscraper puzzles of order 4, 5, 6 and 7 in reading order: the middle is an n×n grid in which each cell is the height of a building (a dot-matrix digit from 1 to n, with a slightly darker and higher base for taller buildings) and every row and column holds 1 to n once each; the ring around it holds the clues giving how many buildings can be seen looking in from that side (taller buildings hide shorter ones behind them, so only buildings taller than all before them are counted). The digits are 3×5 dot matrix on cream paper with thin grid lines and a frame. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved answer with all four sides of clues (a real puzzle gives only the clues and hides the digits inside); the solutions are cyclic Latin squares with row, column and symbol permutations, so they do not cover all possible solutions; the digits 1 to n are printed in dot matrix and the order-7 cells are small (about 0.05 tile). Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`pianoroll` adds a piano roll, bringing the current catalog from 982 to 983 kinds (984 gallery choices including plain). Each tile looks like a piano-roll editor: on the left a keyboard strip of 24 semitones (two octaves) with white keys as bright rows and black keys (C♯, D♯, F♯, G♯ and A♯ of each octave) as dark rows, and on the right a grid of 24 rows × 32 steps (with a more visible beat line every 4 steps) in which a melody is drawn as bright blue bars. The melody uses a major scale picked by the seed (one of the 12 tonics), starts on the tonic, moves one or two scale degrees at a time without repeating a pitch, with notes of 1 to 4 steps until the 32 steps are full; higher notes are brighter. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the melody is a random walk with no rhythmic or harmonic musicality; the pitch range is fixed at 24 semitones (two octaves) with the tonic in the middle-to-upper part; only a major scale is used, with no modulation, chords or rests; the keyboard strip is drawn as bright and dark rows, not as real key shapes. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`binaryclock` adds a binary clock, bringing the current catalog from 983 to 984 kinds (985 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is split into 2×2 clock panels that show four consecutive seconds in reading order: the clock shows hh:mm:ss as six decimal digits in binary coded decimal (BCD), each digit a column of four dots whose bits from top to bottom are 8, 4, 2 and 1, bright green meaning 1 and dark meaning 0. The start time is picked by the seed anywhere in a day (00:00:00 to 23:59:59), so the last column (the units of the seconds) advances from panel to panel, and when the seconds roll over (for example at 59) the carry runs on into the minutes and hours, wrapping from 23:59:59 to 00:00:00. A thin frame surrounds each dark panel. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: it is a 24-hour clock only, with no date or milliseconds; the start time comes from the seed and the nine seeds in the tests do not necessarily include a minute or day rollover (the modulo logic for the carry is written but not covered by these seeds — see ACCEPTANCE); the dots carry no labels for their bit weights (8, 4, 2, 1). Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`othelloboard` adds an Othello board, bringing the current catalog from 984 to 985 kinds (986 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is split into 2×2 green 8×8 boards which, in reading order, are four consecutive positions of a random game of Othello/Reversi, each the result of one more legal move after the previous one: starting from the standard opening (four discs in the centre), black first, the seed drives 14 to 32 random legal moves, and the position after them plus the three that follow are shown; a move places one new disc and flips the runs of opposing discs it brackets along the eight directions, and a player with no legal move passes. Black discs are dark, white discs bright, on a green felt board with thin grid lines in a dark wooden frame. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the game is entirely random legal moves, not a meaningful game, and the positions are not guaranteed to look like a real match (the preview often shows discs in rows or crosses); only four consecutive positions are shown and it is not marked whose turn it is; the test verifies each move's flips with an independent eight-direction ray scan but does not check that the whole game could have legally led from the opening to the first position (only the one-move steps between neighbouring positions). Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`goboards` adds Go boards, bringing the current catalog from 985 to 986 kinds (987 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is split into 2×2 wood-grained 9×9 Go boards (thin grid lines, five star points, dark wooden frame), the four being positions of random legal games after 16, 28, 42 and 58 moves: black and white alternate, black first, each move goes on a random empty point; if it leaves an opposing group with no liberties that group is captured and removed, and if the played stone's own group would have no liberties the move is rejected and another point is tried (no suicide). Black stones are dark discs, white stones bright discs. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the game is entirely random, not a meaningful game or a life-and-death problem; only capture and the no-suicide rule are implemented — there is no ko rule, no passing, scoring or end of game; if 40 tries find no legal point the move is skipped, so a board can have fewer stones than moves; white stones use the brightest end of the colour ramp, which in this material (a single dark-to-light gradient) is cream rather than pure white. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`dewbeads` adds dew drops, bringing the current catalog from 986 to 987 kinds (988 gallery choices including plain). On a dark green leaf with thin slanting veins and a soft mottling lie round dew drops: the tile is split into 12×12 cells and each cell holds a drop with about 62% probability, with a radius between 0.014 and 0.028 of the tile width and a position jittered inside the cell so drops never touch each other or cross the tile edge. A drop's height is a spherical cap (highest at the centre, falling towards the edge as √(1−d²) where d is the distance from the centre over the radius) and its colour is refraction-like: bright in the middle, dark at the rim, with a small highlight towards the upper left; it is smoother than the leaf. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: a drop is just a simple spherical cap, with no real refraction or reflection and no water film or running; positions are bound to the cells, so a faint grid arrangement can still be seen; the leaf is artificial slanting veins plus noise mottling, not a real leaf; the colour is a single dark-to-light ramp (the green hue comes from the preset's dark and light ends). Defaults are a 0.3 m nominal tile, 0.008 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`bowlingscoresheet` adds a bowling score sheet, bringing the current catalog from 987 to 988 kinds (989 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is the score sheet of four games of ten-pin bowling (one row per game, ten frames): the top right of each frame holds the roll boxes — two small boxes in frames 1 to 9 and three in frame 10 — recording each roll in order, a strike as "X", a spare as "/", a miss (0 pins) as "-" and otherwise the number of pins knocked down, 1 to 9; below them is the cumulative score in dot-matrix digits. The rolls are random from the seed (about a 30% chance of a strike or spare on each ball, otherwise any number of the pins left); the cumulative score follows the standard rules: a strike scores 10 plus the next two rolls, a spare 10 plus the next roll, otherwise the pins of the two rolls; frame 10 gives a third roll after a strike or spare, and in frames 1 to 9 a strike is written only in the right-hand box. Cream paper with grey grid lines and dark ink. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the rolls are random and not the distribution of a real bowler (strikes and spares are a fixed 30% each, so scores run high and runs of strikes are common); there are only four games and no player names, dates or title; the notation in frame 10 (for example that a ball clearing the rack after a strike is also written as X) follows common practice and was not checked against any official rulebook; the numbers are 3×5 dot matrix. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.003 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`hexresourceboard` adds a hexagonal resource-island board, bringing the current catalog from 988 to 989 kinds (990 gallery choices including plain). The middle of each tile holds an island of 19 hexagons (arranged 3, 4, 5, 4, 3) surrounded by dark blue sea. The hexagons use six brightness levels for six kinds of terrain, in the numbers 4, 3, 4, 4, 3 and 1 (the brightest kind occurs once, like a desert), with the seed shuffling which kind goes where; each of the 18 hexagons except the desert carries a round number token, using 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 5, 5, 6, 6, 8, 8, 9, 9, 10, 10, 11, 11, 12 once each, with the Catan rule that the two "red" numbers 6 and 8 never sit on adjacent hexagons (the seed's shuffle is retried until that holds). A token is a light disc with a dark dot-matrix number, and the sea has faint swell. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: terrain is told apart only by brightness, with no forests, pastures or other pictures, and the 6s and 8s are not drawn red (this material has a single dark-to-light ramp); the number and terrain layout follow the rules above, but the rules were implemented from memory and not checked against the official rulebook; if 400 shuffles cannot satisfy the "6 and 8 not adjacent" condition the last one is kept (the sampled seeds in the tests never needed that); there is a single island per tile with no special handling for repeating the tile. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.005 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`kakuropuzzle` adds a Kakuro puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 989 to 990 kinds (991 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 8×8 Kakuro grid: the first row and first column are black cells, the seed adds 6 to 9 random black cells inside, and only layouts in which every run of white cells has at least two cells are kept; the white cells are filled with digits 1 to 9 so that no digit repeats within any horizontal or vertical run (a randomised backtracking fill); the black cell just before the start of each run (to its left or above it) is split by a diagonal line, with the across sum at the top right and the down sum at the bottom left (small one- or two-digit dot-matrix numbers). Cream paper and dark black cells with large dark dot-matrix digits in the white cells. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved grid, while a real Kakuro shows only the clues and hides the white-cell digits; the layout comes from random black cells with only the condition that every white run is at least 2 long, so a unique solution and a good puzzle are not guaranteed; the layout search tries up to 6000 times per seed and I only checked that 209 seeds (the 9 test seeds plus 200 extra ones) found a layout; the dot-matrix digits are 3×5 and a two-digit clue is about 0.045 tile wide. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`hashibridges` adds a bridge puzzle (Hashiwokakero), bringing the current catalog from 990 to 991 kinds (992 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved bridge puzzle on a 9×9 lattice: round islands carry dot-matrix numbers 1 to 8 and are joined by horizontal or vertical single or double bridges, each island's number equals the total number of bridges attached to it (a double bridge counts 2), bridges never cross, and all islands are connected. The seed builds the layout: starting from one island, it repeatedly goes in a random direction, leaves at least one empty cell, and puts a new island there joined by a bridge (about 40% double), reaching about 15 islands (limited by the attempt count, so sometimes fewer), then adds random bridges between pairs of islands that are in line with nothing between them (about 30% of such pairs). Cream paper with faint lattice dots, islands as light discs with a thin dark ring and number. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved bridges, while a real puzzle gives only the island numbers and hides the bridges; the generator guarantees connectivity and no crossings but not that this bridge arrangement is the unique solution to these numbers (real puzzles require uniqueness), and sometimes there are fewer than 15 islands; single and double bridges are close together (the two lines of a double bridge are about 0.016 tile apart). Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`slitherlinkloop` adds a Slitherlink puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 991 to 992 kinds (993 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 7×7 Slitherlink board: lattice dots are small circles, one thick dark closed loop runs along the lattice lines with the cells inside it slightly darker, and the middle of every cell holds (a dot-matrix digit 0 to 3) how many of its four sides are part of the loop. The loop is the outline of a randomly grown connected region (about 55% of the cells): the seed drives the growth, and only regions whose outline has 0 or 2 edges at every lattice dot and can be walked in one lap through all loop dots (a single cycle, with no holes or blocks touching only at a corner) are accepted. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the answer (the loop) together with the numbers of all cells, while a real puzzle gives only some numbers and hides the loop; the region comes from random growth and tends to extend from a particular corner (the preview often shows a shape reaching out from the top), and a unique solution is not guaranteed; 7×7 is fixed. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`masyupearls` adds a Masyu puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 992 to 993 kinds (994 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved Masyu on an 8×8 lattice of dots (the corners of 7×7 squares): a closed loop along the lattice lines (again the outline of a randomly grown connected region) with white and black pearls on it. At a white pearl the loop must go straight and at least one of the two neighbouring dots must be a turn; at a black pearl the loop must turn and both arms must go straight through their next dot (so it runs at least one more step each way after turning). All dots that qualify under these rules are collected and the seed takes about 40% of them (at least 5, or all of them if fewer qualify). A white pearl is a light disc with a thin dark ring, a black pearl a dark disc; other dots are small circles. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the answer (the loop) together with the pearls, while a real puzzle gives only the pearls and hides the loop; the pearl rules were implemented from memory of the usual Masyu rules (white goes straight with at least one turning neighbour, black turns with both arms straight) and not checked against a rulebook; pearls are only placed at dots that satisfy the rules, so they are consistent with the loop, but a unique solution to the whole puzzle is not guaranteed; the loop comes from a randomly grown region and tends to extend from a particular corner. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`stringartcurves` adds string art (modular multiplication chord diagrams), bringing the current catalog from 993 to 994 kinds (995 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is split into 2×2 circles: each circle has 96 evenly spaced points numbered 0 to 95, and point i is joined by a straight line to point (k·i mod 96), where k is the circle's multiplier (2, 3, 4 and 5 in the four circles, shuffled between quadrants by the seed, with each circle turned by a small angle of less than one point spacing). The envelope of these lines is a cardioid for k = 2 and a nephroid for k = 3, with more lobes for larger k. Lines add up and are compressed into brightness (1−e^{−1.6·sum}), so one line is about 0.8 bright and overlaps saturate, with a thin dark ring on the circumference on a deep blue-black background. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the envelope is the visual effect of the chords and no curve is drawn separately; with the same number of points, larger multipliers make the chords very dense (the k = 5 circle looks like a blurred rose); fixed points (k·i ≡ i) draw no line; the number of points is fixed at 96. The sampler measures the distance to up to about 95 chords of the pixel's own circle for every pixel, about 223 ms for one 512×512 build, which is slower than most materials and not optimised. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.003 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 512 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`fillominoregions` adds a Fillomino puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 994 to 995 kinds (996 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 9×9 Fillomino board: every cell holds the size of its region (a dot-matrix digit 1 to 9), the cells of one region all show the same number and there are exactly that many of them, regions are separated by thicker dark lines (thin lines inside a region), and larger regions are slightly darker. The seed builds the regions by random growth: in cell order, each unassigned cell gets a random target size 1 to 5 and grows into neighbouring empty cells, and if it ends up the same size as a touching region it keeps adding cells until it differs (so touching regions always differ in size and never merge by accident); if the whole board cannot be completed (about 60% of attempts) it is retried. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved board, while a real Fillomino gives only some numbers; region sizes are limited to 1 to 9 (they can exceed 5 after the "differ from neighbours" growth) and the size distribution leans larger because touching regions must differ; a unique solution is not guaranteed; the digits are 3×5 dot matrix. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`futoshikigrid` adds a Futoshiki puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 995 to 996 kinds (997 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 5×5 Futoshiki board: each of the 25 squares holds a dot-matrix digit 1 to 5 and every row and column has 1 to 5 exactly once (a Latin square: the cyclic Latin square with rows, columns and symbols shuffled); between neighbouring squares an inequality sign is drawn with about 55% probability — "<" and ">" for horizontal neighbours, "∧" and "∨" for vertical ones — with the pointed end (the apex) on the side of the smaller number. The squares are light with a thin frame and leave gaps between them for the signs. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved board, while a real Futoshiki gives only some digits and signs; the signs are derived from the answer and all hold, but a unique solution to the whole puzzle is not guaranteed; the signs are small thin-line symbols (about 0.022 tile wide) that are hard to read at small sizes; 5×5 is fixed. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview.
