`nurikabeislands` adds a Nurikabe puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 996 to 997 kinds (998 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 8×8 Nurikabe board: light cells are "islands" and the deep-blue cells are one connected "sea"; each island carries a dot-matrix number on a single cell, equal to the island's cell count; islands never touch orthogonally, the sea is a single connected region and contains no 2×2 block (a "pool"). The seed builds the board: it places 4 to 7 random islands, fills each 2×2 pool by extending a neighbouring island or adding a one-cell island, then requires the sea to stay connected and every island to be at most 9 cells, retrying with different hashes otherwise (a script simulation suggested about 3% of attempts succeed). Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved board, while a real Nurikabe gives only the island numbers and the solver must deduce the sea; a unique solution is not guaranteed; which cell of an island carries the number (always the cell the island grew from) is seed-driven; 8×8 is fixed and the digits are a 3×5 dot matrix. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`tentsandtrees` adds a Tents puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 997 to 998 kinds (999 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 8×8 Tents board: on grass cells there are round-canopy "trees" with a trunk and triangular "tents"; every tent sits orthogonally next to a tree of its own, tents never touch each other even diagonally, and the number of trees equals the number of tents; a column on the right and a row below the board show each row's and column's tent count as dot-matrix digits. The seed builds the board: tents are placed in random order (not next to an existing tent, diagonals included), and each tent picks an empty orthogonal grass cell for its tree, until about 17 tents are placed or no room is left. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved board, while a real Tents puzzle gives only the trees and the counts and the solver must find the tents; a unique solution is not guaranteed; the tent count is seed-driven and usually below the target of 17 (measured 10 to 16 on 12 seeds); the tent-to-tree pairing has no connecting line and can only be read from adjacency; 8×8 is fixed. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. `shikakurects` adds a Shikaku puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 998 to 999 kinds (1000 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 10×10 Shikaku board: the whole board is cut into rectangles, exactly one cell of each rectangle holds a dot-matrix number equal to the rectangle's cell count, rectangles are separated by thicker dark lines (thin lines inside), and every rectangle has a slightly different random tint. The seed decides the cuts: starting from the whole board, a block is split in two at a random position along its longer side; blocks of area 9 or less stop early with 40% probability (area 2 or less always stops) and larger ones always keep splitting; the number goes on a random cell of the rectangle. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved board, while a real Shikaku gives only the numbers and the solver draws the rectangles; a unique solution is not guaranteed; rectangle areas are limited to 9 (a single dot-matrix digit); 10×10 is fixed and the digits are a 3×5 dot matrix. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. `hitorishading` adds a Hitori puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 999 to 1000 kinds (1001 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 7×7 Hitori board: every cell holds a dot-matrix number 1 to 7, and the blacked-out cells have a dark background with a reversed number; blacked-out cells never touch orthogonally, the remaining cells form one connected body, and the cells that are not blacked out have no repeated number in any row or column; the number of every blacked-out cell equals that of some unblacked cell in its row or column (so blacking it out is actually necessary). The seed builds the board: a 7×7 Latin square with rows, columns and symbols shuffled, then cells are blacked out in random order (not next to an already blacked cell, and only if the rest stays connected) up to about 13, and each blacked-out cell's number is replaced by that of an unblacked cell in its row or column. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved board with the shading drawn, while a real Hitori gives only the numbers and the solver decides which cells to black out; a unique solution is not guaranteed and neither is that this is the only valid shading; the unblacked cells are part of a Latin square, so the board looks more regular than a typical puzzle; the number of blacked-out cells is about 13 (possibly fewer when no more can be added); 7×7 is fixed and the digits are a 3×5 dot matrix. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. `akarilights` adds an Akari (Light Up) puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 1000 to 1001 kinds (1002 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 8×8 Akari board: white cells hold round bulbs, black walls (about 26% of the cells) block the view, each bulb lights its row and column until a wall, every white cell is lit and no two bulbs can see each other; about 60% of the walls carry a reversed dot-matrix number (0 to 4) on the dark background giving how many bulbs touch it orthogonally. The seed builds the board: walls are placed at random, then in random order every white cell that is not lit yet receives a bulb (an unlit cell cannot see an existing bulb, so there is never a conflict) until everything is lit. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved board, while a real Akari gives only the walls and some numbers and the solver deduces the bulbs; a unique solution is not guaranteed; no light rays are drawn; the walls are not required to be connected, and a white cell closed in by walls simply carries its own bulb; 8×8 is fixed and the digits are a 3×5 dot matrix. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. `takuzugrid` adds a Takuzu (Binairo) puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 1001 to 1002 kinds (1003 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 8×8 Takuzu board: every cell is either a filled disc (1) or a hollow ring (0) and the board obeys all Takuzu rules — each row and column has exactly four discs and four rings, no row or column has three equal symbols in a line, no two rows are identical and no two columns are identical. The seed builds the board with backtracking: cells are tried in order (which symbol is tried first comes from a hash) and the search steps back on any rule violation until the board is full. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved board, while a real Takuzu gives only some of the cells; a unique solution is not guaranteed; the backtracking has a node limit (100000 nodes) — across 300 seeds I measured at most about 2400 nodes, far below it, but if a seed ever exceeded the limit the board would be left with unfilled cells drawn as hollow rings, which I did not encounter and did not verify visually; 8×8 is fixed. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`kenkencages` adds a KenKen puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 1002 to 1003 kinds (1004 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 4×4 KenKen board: each of the 16 cells holds a large dot-matrix digit 1 to 4 and every row and column has 1 to 4 exactly once (a Latin square: the cyclic Latin square with rows, columns and symbols shuffled); the board is cut by thick lines into "cages" (connected blocks of 1 to 3 cells, each with a slightly different tint), and each cage carries a small dot-matrix label in the top-left of its first cell (row-major order) giving a target and an operator: a one-cell cage shows just the number; a two-cell cage may use addition (+), multiplication (×), subtraction (−, larger minus smaller) or division (÷, larger divided by smaller, which must divide evenly); a three-cell cage uses addition or multiplication. The operators are 3×5 dot matrices. The targets are computed from the solution, so all of them hold. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved board, while a real KenKen gives only the cages and targets (not the answer); a unique solution is not guaranteed (I did not write a solver to check); cages have at most 3 cells; 4×4 is fixed; both the large digits and the small labels are dot matrices that are hard to read at small sizes. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`numberlinkpaths` adds a Numberlink puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 1003 to 1004 kinds (1005 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 6×6 Numberlink board: about 6 to 9 pairs of endpoints, each a dark disc holding a reversed dot-matrix number (1 to 9), every pair joined by one thick line, and the paths together fill all 36 cells exactly, never crossing, branching or forming loops; different paths have slightly different line tones. The seed builds the board: horizontal dominoes tile the board and many random 2×2 rotations produce a random domino tiling, then dominoes of different paths whose ends touch are joined end to end until about 6 to 9 paths remain. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the solved board, while a real Numberlink gives only the endpoint numbers; a unique solution is not guaranteed; paths tend to be short and winding (they are built from dominoes); 6×6 is fixed and the digits are a 3×5 dot matrix; if the joining ever stalled with more than 9 paths the numbers would run past the glyph table (I did not meet this across 300 seeds plus the nine test seeds, but did not prove it cannot happen). Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. `rushhourjam` adds a Rush Hour board, bringing the current catalog from 1004 to 1005 kinds (1006 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a 6×6 sliding-vehicle board: a dark target car (two cells, horizontal, in the exit row), about ten more cars (length 2, about 70%) and trucks (length 3), horizontal or vertical, drawn as rounded rectangles in slightly different greys, and a dark wall around the board that is open in the exit row on the right. Vehicles never overlap, and no horizontal vehicle is placed in the exit row (it would block the target car for good). The seed builds the layout: the target car gets a random starting column, the other vehicles are tried at random positions and orientations and kept when they do not overlap, until about 11 vehicles (target included) are placed or the attempts run out. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile is just one board state and is **not guaranteed solvable** (I wrote no solver; vertical vehicles can still lock the target car in for good) and not a hard puzzle; colour is brightness only (a dark target car and grey vehicles), no hue; vehicles are plain rounded rectangles without windows or lights; 6×6 is fixed and the exit is fixed on the right of the third row. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. `mancalaboard` adds a Mancala board, bringing the current catalog from 1005 to 1006 kinds (1007 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a wooden Mancala board: a rounded board on a light tabletop, an elongated "store" at each end and two rows of six round "pits" between them, the pits and stores being dark recesses holding small pale round stones. There are 48 stones in total, distributed at random by the seed over the 12 pits and 2 stores (a pit holds at most 12 and a store at most 24; a full container takes no more); inside each container the stones are laid out from the centre outwards. The tabletop and board carry a slight wood-grain brightness variation (noise stretched horizontally). Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic, not a rules check: the distribution is random and does not represent a legal Mancala game state (a real game's stone distribution depends on the moves played); no player marks or direction of play are drawn; the stones are small (radius about 0.0125 of the tile) and hard to read at small sizes; colour is brightness only (a linear wood-tone ramp from dark to light). `hidatochain` adds a Hidato puzzle, bringing the current catalog from 1006 to 1007 kinds (1008 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a solved 7×7 Hidato board: each of the 49 cells holds a dot-matrix number 1 to 49 (single digits centred, two digits side by side in 3×5 glyphs), every number appears exactly once, and consecutive numbers k and k+1 are always neighbours horizontally, vertically or diagonally (a king's move), so 1 to 49 form one path through the whole board. The start "1" and end "49" are drawn on a dark background with reversed digits, and about 22% of the other cells have a slightly darker background (imitating "given" numbers). The seed finds the path with backtracking: starting from a random cell, each step prefers the neighbour with the fewest free cells to continue to (Warnsdorff's rule, ties decided by a hash). Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the tile shows the board with every number filled in, while a real Hidato gives only some numbers; the slightly darker "given" cells are random decoration and say nothing about whether a puzzle would have a unique solution; a unique solution is not guaranteed; the backtracking has a node limit (100000) — it found a path for 300 seeds plus the nine test seeds (an independent script measured at most about 2650 nodes), and if the limit were exceeded the board would stop on an unfinished path, which I did not verify visually; 7×7 is fixed. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. `euclideanrhythms` adds Euclidean rhythms, bringing the current catalog from 1007 to 1008 kinds (1009 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a 16-step by 16-track step-sequencer panel: a dark panel carrying 16×16 rounded-square pads, a lit pad meaning a pulse on that step. Every track is a "Euclidean rhythm" — k pulses (k chosen by the seed between 1 and 8) spread as evenly as possible over 16 steps (a Bresenham-style integer spread: step i is lit exactly when (i×k) mod 16 < k), then rotated by a seed-driven offset; so the gap between any two consecutive pulses (wrapping around) is only ever ⌊16/k⌋ or ⌈16/k⌉, with exactly 16 mod k gaps of the larger kind. Unlit pads on every fourth step (columns 1, 5, 9, 13) are slightly lighter, marking the beats. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: it is a static pattern with no audio; this Bresenham-style spread is a rotation of the Bjorklund algorithm's rhythm (the same necklace), but I did not prove that for every k and I make no claim that any track equals a particular traditional rhythm by name; 16×16 is fixed; colour is brightness only (a dark panel, grey pads, lit cream pads). Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. This description does not claim three-backend or cross-browser verification.

`chinesecheckersboard` adds a Chinese checkers board, bringing the current catalog from 1008 to 1009 kinds (1010 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a wooden Chinese checkers star board: a round board plate on a light tabletop, carrying a six-pointed star of 121 round holes — a central hexagon of radius 4 (61 holes) and a 10-hole triangle on each of the six points. The holes are dark recesses holding marbles of six brightness levels (dark to light: about 0.30, 0.42, 0.55, 0.69, 0.83, 0.96); each point starts with ten marbles of one level, and then the seed moves 14 marbles, each to a random empty hole, so every level still has exactly 10 marbles and there are 60 in total. Holes map to a honeycomb lattice through cube coordinates (q, r, s=−q−r): the star is the union of two opposite triangles, "all three coordinates ≥ −4" and "all three ≤ 4". Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the six marble kinds differ by brightness only, with no hue (the real game has six colours), and neighbouring levels (about 0.12 to 0.14 apart) are hard to tell apart at small sizes or under different lighting; the moves are random swaps and do not represent legal jump moves (the jumping rules are not checked); each player's goal corner is not marked; the wood grain is only low-amplitude noise. Defaults are a 0.6 m nominal tile, 0.006 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. `colormapregions` adds a map-colouring pattern, bringing the current catalog from 1009 to 1010 kinds (1011 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a seamlessly tiling "map": 6×6 randomly jittered points seed a periodic Voronoi partition (36 regions, with thin darker border lines), and every region gets one brightness such that **no two neighbouring regions (including neighbours that meet across the tile edge, onto the next tile) share a brightness**. The colouring uses backtracking: four brightnesses are tried first and, failing that (node limit 30000), five (0.30, 0.44, 0.58, 0.72, 0.86); over 100 seeds I measured 66 using only four, 34 needing five, and none failing to colour. Neighbours are found by the sampler scanning a periodic 512×512 grid for owner changes (diagonals included). Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: because the map lives on a torus (left meets right, top meets bottom) it is not covered by the planar four-colour theorem — four colours are not always enough, about a third of the seeds need five (measured above), and the name does not mean only four are used; neighbours are detected by scanning, so a border shorter than about 0.002 of the tile could in principle be missed (rescanning seeds 2 and 3 on a 1024 grid found none missing, but I did not prove this for all seeds); colour is brightness only. Building the sampler costs about 32 ms extra (mean over 100 seeds, measured in Bun, not a performance certification). Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. Existing periodic opaque maps, optional height, tint/repeats, Worker/cache APIs and texture ownership are reused without new APIs or dependencies. Prefer 1024 or larger; 32 is a coarse preview. `draughtsposition` adds a draughts (checkers) board, bringing the current catalog from 1010 to 1011 kinds (1012 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is an 8×8 draughts board: a checkered board of light and dark brown squares on a light tabletop, with pieces only on the dark squares (those where row + column is odd). Each side has 6 to 12 pieces (random per seed), one side pale discs and the other charcoal discs; about a fifth of the pieces are kings, with a concentric ring of the opposite tone in the middle of the disc. Positions are chosen by the seed: the 32 dark squares are shuffled and the two sides take consecutive runs, so pieces never overlap or land on a light square. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic, not a legal game state: the positions are random and need not match any move history (a man may stand on the far rank without being crowned, and the two sides may be mixed together); it does not say whose turn it is; colour is brightness only (pale pieces contrast little with the light squares, and charcoal pieces have limited contrast with the dark squares); the wood grain is only low-amplitude noise. Defaults are a 0.5 m nominal tile, 0.006 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0. `multiplicationtable` adds a times table, bringing the current catalog from 1011 to 1012 kinds (1013 gallery choices including plain). Each tile is a 9×9 multiplication table: the first row and column are the factors 1 to 9 on a dark background with reversed dot-matrix digits (the top-left corner is blank), and the other 81 cells hold the matching products (1 to 81, single digits centred, two digits side by side in 3×5 glyphs); the square numbers on the diagonal (1, 4, 9, …, 81) have a slightly darker background. The table sits on pale paper with thin grid lines. The content is fixed and does not change with the seed; the seed only affects the faint paper noise. Metallic and mortar are 0.

This is an authored schematic: the factors stop at 9 (not the common 12×12 table); because the content is fixed, different seeds differ only by noise; the dot-matrix digits are hard to read at small sizes. Defaults are a 0.4 m nominal tile, 0.004 UV-relative relief (not metres of displacement) and metallic factor 0.
